34	\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}
35	\label{sec:agent-improvement}
36	
37	\begin{table}[t]
38	\centering
39	\small
40	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
41	\toprule
42	Condition & Death\% & $N$ \\
43	\midrule
44	Single-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\
45	Reflexion ($3{\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\
46	Qwen2.5-72B$^\ddagger$ & 33.7 & 175 \\
47	Deterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\
48	\midrule
49	\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\
50	Death-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\
51	Retry ($5{\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\
52	\bottomrule
53	\end{tabular}
54	\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under agent improvement methods. Top: all tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded, 0\% death at all scales). $\ddagger$\,72B covers 9 of 11 task types (175 episodes); two tasks excluded due to resource constraints. All interventions reduce death rate but none approaches deterministic-level safety.}
55	\label{tab:agent-improvement}
56	\end{table}
57	
58	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\%$ vs.\ $48.0\%$ baseline, $\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\sim}$35\%. Under Reflexion ($N{=}3$, matched-episode analysis), $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF $> 100\%$: the deterministic advantage is entirely attributable to death avoidance, and in non-death episodes the self-correcting agent slightly outperforms the deterministic baseline. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ parameters; both internal and deterministic strategies independently collected at 72B) reduces death rate to $33.7\%$ across 9 task types (175 episodes; \texttt{l

_source line 6686 · 2026-05-19T12:15:02.554895+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We presented a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents via episode-level counterfactual analysis. Applied to ScienceWorld (245 episodes, 11 task types), 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry; agent improvement baselines (retry, $10{\times}$ scaling, Reflexion) confirm this is robust. Cross-domain contrasts support framework predictions: MATH shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism. Replication in permadeath environments (NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020}, Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022}), building on recent LLM agent benchmarks \citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}, and evaluation with stronger backbones are the most direct open directions.
4

58	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\%$ vs.\ $48.0\%$ baseline, $\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\sim}$35\%. Under Reflexion ($N{=}3$, matched-episode analysis), $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF $> 100\%$: the deterministic advantage is entirely attributable to death avoidance, and in non-death episodes the self-correcting agent slightly outperforms the deterministic baseline. _source line 6695 · 2026-05-19T12:47:55.735651+00:00_

7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	While death dominance itself follows from the penalty structure (\S\ref{sec:daf}), its resistance to intervention was not predictable a priori. Four representative axes spanning retry, self-correction, parameter scaling, and action-space restriction all fail to close the gap to deterministic safety (16.3\%): Retry ($43.5\%$ at $N{=}5$), Reflexion ($34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$, plateauing at $34$--$36.5\%$), $10{\times}$ scaling ($33.7\%$, 72B), and action-space filtering ($29.0\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). Action filtering and 72B scaling yield the strongest reductions yet still leave ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps. Reflexion and scaling converge to overlapping 95\% Wilson CIs ($[27.8\%, 40.8\%]$ and $[28.0\%, 41.0\%]$); this overlap suggests a shared bound from the lack of ground-truth state access. The oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\% of decisions (score improvement: 2.89 points), and even with perfect per-task death risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}). More aggressive planning-based interventions (Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested and may further reduce death rates; we characterize a ceiling for the tested intervention classes, not a universal bound.
9

_source line 6719 · 2026-05-19T13:13:14.216571+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Patch — MiniHack/Crafter Negative Contrast 写入 Discussion]

D125 终止了第三环境 permadeath replication。MiniHack 和 Crafter 的 pilot 结果虽未产生 death asymmetry，但可作为 negative contrast 增强 Discussion。

**数据：**
- MiniHack 导航（Room-5x5, Room-15x15, Corridor-R3, Corridor-R5, River）：5 tasks × 2 conditions, 全部 0% real death (end_status=1)。导航任务无怪物生成，结构性无 death mechanism。
- Corridor-R3/R5 有 information asymmetry（Det 40-60% goal vs LLM 0%），但无 death → 无 death routing signal。这与 ALFWorld 模式一致。
- Crafter：Det 100% death, LLM 100% death (0 achievements), Random 100% death。环境太难，即使 rule-based agent 有 20-tile 全图访问也 100% 死亡。无 asymmetry。

**写入位置：Discussion 或 Limitations**

新增 1-2 句即可，不要膨胀。建议 framing：

"Preliminary pilots on MiniHack navigation (0\% death rate, no monster spawning) and Crafter (100\% death rate for both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: environments lacking a death mechanism yield no routing signal (consistent with the MATH null result), while environments where even oracle-informed strategies cannot avoid death also lack exploitable asymmetry. These results underscore that the framework's diagnostic power requires an intermediate regime where strategy choice meaningfully affects survival."

**Framing 硬约束（Reviewer 已警告）：**
- ✗ 不要说 "MiniHack permadeath environment shows no death asymmetry"（MiniHack 导航不是 permadeath，没有怪物）
- ✓ 说 "navigation tasks lacking death mechanism"
- 不要 overclaim "permadeath environments don't necessarily produce asymmetry"

**页面约束：** 正文 ≤ 8 页。如果空间紧张，缩到一句或放 Appendix。

1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}
5	The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable features. Cross-task-type transfer to physical-science tasks yields chance-level accuracy (49.2\%, 95\% CI $[42.4\%, 55.9\%]$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}), and 7 of 11 task types contain only deterministic-preferred labels, making majority-class collapse a structural consequence of the label distribution.
6	
7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	While death dominance itself follows from the penalty structure (\S\ref{sec:daf}), its resistance to intervention was not predictable a priori. Four representative axes spanning retry, self-correction, parameter scaling, and action-space restriction all fail to close the gap to deterministic safety (16.3\%): Retry ($43.5\%$ at $N{=}5$), Reflexion ($34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$, plateauing at $34$--$36.5\%$), $10{\times}$ scaling ($31.4\%$, 72B, $N{=}255$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). Action filtering and 72B scaling yield the strongest reductions yet still leave ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps. Reflexion and scaling converge to overlapping 95\% Wilson CIs ($[27.8\%, 40.8\%]$ and $[26.0\%, 37.3\%]$); this overlap suggests a shared bound from the lack of ground-truth state access. The oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\% of decisions (score improvement: 2.89 points), and even with perfect per-task death risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform always-determi

  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The framework requires paired modality episodes with binary catastrophic outcomes. Preliminary pilots on MiniHack navigation (0\\% death rate, no monster spawning) and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} (100\\% death rate for both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: environments lacking a death mechanism yield no routing signal (consistent with the MATH null), while environments where no strategy avoids death lack exploitable asymmetry. The framework's diagnostic power requires an intermediate regime where strategy choice meaningfully affects survival; NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks, where permadeath interacts with strategy quality, remain the most direct replication target; recent benchmarks \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide infrastructure."
}
```

</details>

_source line 6745 · 2026-05-19T13:15:07.573938+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We presented a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents via episode-level counterfactual analysis. Applied to ScienceWorld (245 episodes, 11 task types), 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry; agent improvement baselines (retry, self-correction, $10{\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) confirm this is robust. Cross-domain contrasts support framework predictions: MATH shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism. Replication in permadeath environments (NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020}, Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022}), building on recent LLM agent benchmarks \citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}, and evaluation with stronger backbones are the most direct open directions.
4

_source line 6756 · 2026-05-19T14:00:16.230239+00:00_

7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	While death dominance itself follows from the penalty structure (\S\ref{sec:daf}), its resistance to intervention was not predictable a priori. Four representative axes spanning retry, self-correction, parameter scaling, and action-space restriction all fail to close the gap to deterministic safety (16.3\%): Retry ($43.5\%$ at $N{=}5$), Reflexion ($34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$, plateauing at $34$--$36.5\%$), $10{\times}$ scaling ($31.4\%$, 72B, $N{=}255$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). Action filtering and 72B scaling yield the strongest reductions yet still leave ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps. Reflexion and scaling converge to overlapping 95\% Wilson CIs ($[27.8\%, 40.8\%]$ and $[26.0\%, 37.3\%]$); this overlap suggests a shared bound from the lack of ground-truth state access. The oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\% of decisions (score improvement: 2.89 points), and even with perfect per-task death risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}). More aggressive planning-based interventions (Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested and may further reduce death rates; we characterize a ceiling for the tested intervention classes, not a universal bound.
9

[Director 指令] [Patch — R18 修复，分三个优先级批次]

R18 Overall 3.0，需修复到 3.5。以下是综合 Reviewer + Research Reviewer 审核后的最终 Action Matrix。

## P0 — Must-Fix（不做无法提分）

### MF1: DAF framing 结构性修复（R13-N1 反复回归的根因是 §3.1 文本顺序）

1. **§3.1 首句必须以 78.9% 开头**：
   - 当前结构：先报告 "Δ_full = +29.60... DAF = 90.4%"，78.9% 在后半段
   - 修改为：首句 "Of 95 non-tie routing labels, 75 (78.9%) arise from death asymmetry..."
   - DAF 移到段落中后部："Under ScienceWorld's penalty structure (P=100), this translates to DAF = 90.4% (95% CI [81.6%, 98.8%])"

2. **§3.1 增加 DAF vs P inline 数据**（具体数值等 Agent 回复后填入，先预留位置）：
   "DAF varies with penalty magnitude: [P=10: X%, P=25: Y%, P=50: Z%, P=100: 90.4%], confirming that the 78.9% label fraction—which is penalty-independent—provides the more robust characterization."

3. **Abstract 确认**：当前已以 78.9% 领衔，不需改。

### MF2: Episode→step gap 正式讨论

**§2 末尾增加 "Scope of Episode-Level Analysis" 段落**（约 6-8 行）：
核心论点：
- 97.4% step-level tie rate（Algorithm 1 在 step-level 上几乎所有步骤产生 tie）→ episode-level 是唯一能产生有效 routing signal 的粒度
- 框架的诊断输出是 "此环境是否 death-dominated"（binary diagnostic），不是 step-level 训练信号
- 27.7% episode-level mislabeling（Appendix B）量化了 episode→step projection 的信息损失
- 明确声明：这是 diagnostic tool，不是 step-level routing optimizer

**§3.6 增加一句**："Training and validation use episode-level splits (Appendix J), ensuring no within-episode information leakage."

## P1 — Should-Fix（影响评分但不一票否决）

### SF1: ALFWorld information asymmetry 定量
**§3.4 增加一句**（数值等 Agent 确认）：
"Of 37 non-tie labels, 35 (94.6%) involve the deterministic strategy's access to admissible actions—an information asymmetry rather than a survival advantage, demonstrating that the framework diagnoses environment-specific mechanisms without modification."

### SF3: Non-triviality 重写
**§5 "Non-triviality of death dominance" 段从三个角度重写**：
1. **Routing prior**：现有 routing 工作（Toolformer/ART/Chameleon/CogRouter）假设 signal 来自 diverse capability differences。无人预测过单一 binary failure mode 主导 >78% 信号。
2. **Intervention resistance**：Reflexion Δ_nd = -0.47 说明 self-correction 帮助非死亡 episodes，但 death dominance 完整保留——不能从 "death is hard" 共识推导出这个结果。
3. **Cross-model stability**：δ ≈ 26-31pp 跨三模型稳定，internal vulnerability ~42% 近 model-independent。

### SF2: Cluster bootstrap 主次调整
**§3.1**：以 non-clustered episode-level bootstrap 为 primary inference，cluster bootstrap 重新标注为 "conservative sensitivity analysis (Cameron et al., 2008)"。Cohen's d=3.02 提升至正文。

### SF4: 63% task exclusion scope
**§3 开头段**确认是否已有此讨论，如无，增加："Our analysis covers 11 of 30 task types (37%), selected by methodological criteria (Table 3). The excluded 19 tasks show even stronger death-rate differentials (+38.2pp), ruling out cherry-picking but underscoring scope limitation."

### SF5: Router confounds
**§3.6**：增加 train/val episode-level split 说明（见 MF2）。
Compounding error 在 Limitations 中 acknowledge："Step-level routing failures may reflect compounding errors rather than binary signal limitations alone."

### SF6: Reflexion 统计功效
**§3.3 或 Figure 3 caption** 增加一句（数值等 Agent 确认）：
"Reflexion at N=5 requires approximately 5× the per-episode compute cost; the flat curve at N≥3 is detectable given minimum detectable effect size of ~7pp (paired McNemar, α=0.05, power=0.80, N=200)."

### SF7: Safe RL 段扩展
**§4 safe RL 段**扩展至 3-5 句。补充 Moldovan & Abbeel (2012) safe exploration 形式化（irreversible state avoidance）。添加 bib 条目。

## P2 — Minor + References

### m1: Figure 2 caption
添加："The near-linear relationship is a structural consequence of the DAF decomposition (Eq. 4), not an independent empirical validation."

### m2: "modality" → "computation mode"
全文搜索替换 "modality" → "computation mode"（仅指 internal/deterministic strategy 的地方）。

### m3: Webb (2023) 年份
核实 Webb 引用：如果是 2014 working paper → 改为 Webb (2014)，如果 2023 有正式发表版 → 保留。

### m4: Appendix 索引
Appendix 开头增加简短目录："Appendix A: SCI / B: Mislabeling / C: Per-task / ... / R: Decision Framework"

### m5: "54 sole-identified labels"
§3.1 首次出现处增加括号定义："54 sole-identified labels (episodes where only one strategy produced a non-zero score)"

### m6: Appendix N
增加一句："Failure categories were assigned by a single annotator following the protocol described in §2.2."

### m7: Single action pattern
§5 增加 2-3 句 "Anatomy of catastrophic failure"：100% internal deaths 来自 `focus on [object]` 误用 → signal trivially binary at action level → 但 episode-level 76% 的 death episodes 中 agent 在非致命步骤也做出有效推理 → death avoidance 不等于完全能力缺失。

### Missing References（添加 bib 条目）
1. Moldovan & Abbeel (2012) "Safe Exploration in MDPs" — ICML
2. Ruan et al. (2024) — LLM agent failure taxonomy（需 WebSearch 确认完整引用信息）
3. Graves (2016) "Adaptive Computation Time for RNNs" — arXiv
4. 近期 catastrophic action prevention 工作（需 WebSearch）

## 注意事项
- 有些数值（DAF vs P 曲线、ALFWorld fraction、McNemar p 值）正在等 Agent 回复，标 [TBD] 先预留位置
- 72B 30.6% 修正上一轮已完成，确认无回归
- 编译确认 0 critical warnings + 8 页 main text
- 这次不用验证引用

14	
15	Across 245 episodes, $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$; at the default $P{=}100$). The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction is 78.9\% (75 of 95 non-tie labels). Given observed $\delta = 0.265$ and $P = 100$, the predicted DAF $= \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}) = 26.5 / 29.37 \approx 90.3\%$, closely matching the observed value; DAF is thus a derived quantity once $\delta$, $P$, and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ are known, while the 78.9\% label fraction is the penalty-independent empirical finding. Of these 75, 54 (72.0\%) reduce to ties when the death penalty is removed entirely: in those episodes, both modalities score zero without death, so the only reason one strategy outscored the other was avoiding the $-100$ penalty. Death avoidance is not merely the dominant but often the \emph{sole} discriminating signal between modalities. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score, but the death penalty still accounts for the majority of the score gap. At $P{=}0$, the 54 sole-signal labels reduce to ties, leaving 41 non-tie labels of which 21 (51.2\%) still involve death asymmetry; the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}), but the practical routing value of death avoidance requires $P > 0$.

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.
5	
6	\subsection{Problem Setup}
7	\label{sec:problem-setup}
8	
9	Consider an interactive environment with observation space $\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\pi: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modalities $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$: \textbf{internal} (${\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\cite{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \textbf{deterministic} (${\tt det}$), where the agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step. A \emph{routing policy} $\rho: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{M}$ selects which modality to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\rho$. A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}).
10	
11	\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}
12	\label{sec:collection}
13	
14	Per-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-20
…[truncated 6032 chars]

_source line 6784 · 2026-05-19T15:09:34.334983+00:00_

100	\label{tab:baselines}
101	\end{table}
102	
103	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic, an \emph{oracle degeneracy}: the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.
104	
105	\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}
106	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
107	
108	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; ${\sim}27.7\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with episode-level assignments, further limiting learnability. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes.
109	
110	\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\%$--$800\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\ref{app:ablations}).
111

401	\section{Penalty Sensitivity Analysis}
402	\label{app:penalty}
403	
404	\begin{table}[h]
405	\centering
406	\small
407	\begin{tabular}{@{}rrr@{}}
408	\toprule
409	$P$ & DAF($P$) & 95\% CI \\
410	\midrule
411	0 & 7.5\% & [$-36.7$, $83.1$] \\
412	10 & 50.4\% & [$19.8$, $94.5$] \\
413	25 & 70.8\% & [$48.5$, $97.1$] \\
414	50 & 82.6\% & [$67.6$, $98.4$] \\
415	100\textsuperscript{*} & 90.4\% & [$81.5$, $99.1$] \\

418	\bottomrule

420	\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. DAF$(P) = 1 - \Delta_{\text{nd}} / \Delta_{\text{full}}(P)$, where $\Delta_{\text{full}}(P) \approx \delta \cdot P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$, $\delta = 0.2653$ is the death-rate differential, and $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (strict neither-died filtering). Bootstrap 95\% CIs from 1{,}000 resamples. \textsuperscript{*}Default setting.}
421	\label{tab:penalty-sensitivity}
422	\end{table}
423	
424	The non-death score gap $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant across penalty levels; it depends only on surviving-episode performance, not on $P$. As $P$ increases, $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ grows ($16.33$ at $P{=}50$, $29.60$ at $P{=}100$, $56.13$ at $P{=}200$) while $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ remains fixed, mechanically increasing DAF.
425	
426	\paragraph{Mechanism boundary: death specificity.}
427	The $r = 0.97$ correlation between per-task death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ is specific to catastrophic death (score $= -100$). Broader failure definitions dissolve the correlation entirely: defining ``failure'' as any non-positive score ($T{=}0$) yields $r = -0.13$ ($p = 0.70$), because non-death episodes show near-zero performance differential between strategies ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$). Table~\ref{tab:penalty-correlation} quantifies this specificity across penalty magnitudes.

  "old_string": "We also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.",
  "new_string": "We also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nThe 97.4\\% step-level tie rate (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}) means episode-level comparison is the only granularity at which routing signal emerges. This design choice has a structural consequence: the framework outputs a binary diagnostic (``is this environment death-dominated?'') rather than step-level routing decisions. When episode-level labels are projected back to steps for router training (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}), ${\\sim}27.7\\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with episode outcomes, quantifying the information loss inherent in this projection. The framework is thus a diagnostic tool for characterizing when routing adds value, not a step-level routing optimizer."
}

72	\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}
73	\label{sec:math-contrast}
74	\label{sec:cross-env}
75	
76	We evaluate two additional environments as boundary probes (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-env-details}). ``Deterministic'' denotes qualitatively different modalities per environment: ScienceWorld uses an oracle simulator with ground-truth state, MATH uses a Python code interpreter, and ALFWorld uses admissible-action enumeration. These are not equivalent computation modes; results probe framework applicability rather than replicate findings.
77	
78	\paragraph{MATH.} On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$, code interpreter as deterministic modality), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp), with balanced labels (107 vs.\ 95, 74.6\% no-preference). Without catastrophic failure, no routing signal emerges.
79	
80	\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\citep{alfworld-2021} (6 task types, 135 games) provides the deterministic strategy with admissible actions, an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch. The success rate gap is $27.4\%$ vs.\ $3.0\%$ (35 det-preferred, 2 int-preferred, 98 no-preference). The deterministic advantage stems from this information gap, a qualitatively different mechanism that the framework diagnoses without modification.
81	
82	\subsection{Baselines}
83	\label{sec:baselines}

24	
25	\paragraph{Sensitivity and seed robustness.}
26	Episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\% CI $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$ for DAF (Cohen's $d = 3.02$); leave-one-task-out analysis confirms stability (DAF range: 75.9\%--78.9\%; Appendix~\ref{app:loto-daf}). An independent seed replication ($N{=}220$) yields DAF $= 89.7\%$, within 0.7~pp of the primary analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}). Cluster-robust inference with $K{=}11$ task-type clusters ($p < 0.001$; primary CIs are episode-level, cluster CIs serve as sensitivity analysis) and 28-task directional consistency (100\%) confirm significance (Appendices~\ref{app:wild-cluster}--\ref{app:per-task-ci}).
27

_source line 6812 · 2026-05-19T15:14:09.693846+00:00_

7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	While death dominance itself follows from the penalty structure (\S\ref{sec:daf}), its resistance to intervention was not predictable a priori. Four representative axes spanning retry, self-correction, parameter scaling, and action-space restriction all fail to close the gap to deterministic safety (16.3\%): Retry ($43.5\%$ at $N{=}5$), Reflexion ($34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$, plateauing at $34$--$36.5\%$), $10{\times}$ scaling ($30.6\%$, 72B, $N{=}255$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). Action filtering and 72B scaling yield the strongest reductions yet still leave ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps. Reflexion and scaling converge to overlapping 95\% Wilson CIs ($[27.8\%, 40.8\%]$ and $[25.2\%, 36.5\%]$); this overlap suggests a shared bound from the lack of ground-truth state access. The oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\% of decisions (score improvement: 2.89 points), and even with perfect per-task death risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}). More aggressive planning-based interventions (Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested and may further reduce death rates; we characterize a ceiling for the tested intervention classes, not a universal bound.

1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types (63\%) lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). This scope limits generalizability: conclusions apply to the included subset, not to all ScienceWorld tasks. An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\Delta\text{DR} = +38.2$\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}).

_source line 6824 · 2026-05-19T15:14:47.948128+00:00_

33	\section*{Limitations}
34	
35	\paragraph{Environmental scope.}
36	Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface non-death-dominated signal that our current setup does not capture. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\S\ref{sec:cross-env}, \S\ref{sec:discussion}) provide preliminary supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct cross-model replication (11 task types, $N{=}220$; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}) shows death dominance replicates with direction consistency 10/10 while vulnerability magnitude varies ($+30.0$~pp vs.\ Qwen $+26.5$~pp), though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ scale) reduces death rate but does not eliminate death dominance (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); validation beyond 72B remains future work. Our quantitative results (DAF $= 90.4\%$, label fractions) are specific to ScienceWorld; the framework's generality claim is methodological (the diagnostic procedure applies to any environment with asymmetric fatal outcomes), not empirical (the specific percentages may differ across environments).
37	
38	\paragraph{Methodological constraints.}
39	Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\citep{react-2023}. Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death rate from 42.9\% to 31.4\% (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), but more aggressive self-correction or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\citep{tot-2023} may further reduce internal death rates. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, which may be suboptimal for

_source line 6828 · 2026-05-19T15:14:59.560236+00:00_

59	
60	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\%$ vs.\ $48.0\%$ baseline, $\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\sim}$35\%. Under Reflexion ($N{=}3$, matched-episode analysis), $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF $> 100\%$: the deterministic advantage is entirely attributable to death avoidance, and in non-death episodes the self-correcting agent slightly outperforms the deterministic baseline. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ parameters; both internal and deterministic strategies independently collected at 72B, 255 episodes across all 11 task types) reduces death rate to $30.6\%$. The 72B deterministic strategy achieves $2.75\%$ death rate ($\Delta\text{DR} = +27.8$~pp), and $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.28$, yielding DAF $= 92.4\%$ (7B: $90.4\%$); scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance intact.
61	
62	\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}
63	We extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\% \to 43.5\%$, $\Delta = -4.5$~pp over five trials). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p < 0.005$) then plateaus ($34$--$36.5\%$ for $N \geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$). Retry's per-trial conditional death rate stays constant at ${\sim}48\%$; Reflexion's declines to ${\sim}31\%$ by Trial~3 (per-task breakdown in Appendix~\ref{app:dose-response}).
64

1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}
4	A growing body of work augments language models with external tools \citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.
5	\emph{Self-supervised} approaches learn tool invocation from loss signals: Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls filtered by perplexity reduction; ToolACE \cite{toolace-2025} curates synthetic data for function-calling accuracy.
6	\emph{Token-level} methods embed tools into the model vocabulary: ToolkenGPT \cite{toolkengpt-2023} represents tools as special tokens resolved at inference.
7	\emph{Task-level} decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules: ART \cite{art-2023} generates tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2023} composes heterogeneous modules per subtask.
8	\emph{RL-based} methods learn routing policies from reward: ReTool \cite{retool-2025} discovers when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought.
9	All focus on \emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}
10	Recent concurrent work sharpens this question: \citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} identify a cognitive-illusion effect where LLMs default to external tools even when internal knowledge suffices, finding that tool overuse correlates with model uncertainty rather than task difficulty. \citet{when2tool-2026} show tool-call decisions are encoded in hidden states before reasoning, \citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax,'' and \citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity, utility, and affordability framework. Our decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\% of routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.
11	
12	\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}
13	CogRouter \cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \cite{mixture-o

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\nSafe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021} enforce constraints in MDPs; SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails. Our diagnostic shows modality routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism in environments with catastrophic failure.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\nSafe RL methods enforce constraints in MDPs through diverse mechanisms: comprehensive surveys \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive} categorize approaches; risk-sensitive objectives \\citep{tamar2015cvar} optimize conditional value-at-risk; constrained policy optimization \\citep{achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021} satisfies cost constraints during training; safe exploration \\citep{moldovan2012safe} guarantees constraint satisfaction during learning. SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails. These methods assume the agent can learn to avoid catastrophic states; our results show that in environments with irreversible failure, modality routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism---the deterministic strategy sidesteps catastrophic states entirely, achieving what constrained optimization would target."
}
```

_source line 6852 · 2026-05-19T15:16:41.061203+00:00_

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.
5	
6	\subsection{Problem Setup}
7	\label{sec:problem-setup}
8	
9	Consider an interactive environment with observation space $\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\pi: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modalities $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$: \textbf{internal} (${\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\cite{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \textbf{deterministic} (${\tt det}$), where the agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step. A \emph{routing policy} $\rho: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{M}$ selects which modality to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\rho$. A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}).
10

29	We use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two modalities can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-env}.

_source line 6868 · 2026-05-19T15:17:13.961671+00:00_

15	Once death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}).
16	
17	This framework addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\% of ScienceWorld steps produce identical scores regardless of modality; \S\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\S\ref{sec:baselines}). Our counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes. The framework applies to any environment with controllable modality comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes.
18	
19	Two cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.
20	First, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality) confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$).
21	Second, a cross-model replication with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (11 task types, $N{=}220$) shows death dominance replicates across model families with direction consistency 10/10 ($+30.0$~pp vs.\ $+26.5$~pp for Qwen; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}).
22	Four intervention axes test whether death dominance is fragile: retry, Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\times}$ parameter scaling (72B), and action-space restriction all reduce death rates, yet none closes the gap to deterministic safety (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}). Action filtering (blocking the fatal action during exploration) and 72B scaling yield the strongest reductions yet still leave ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps, results not predictable from the penalty structure alone.

92	\end{algorithm}
93	
94	A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine modality differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \texttt{no\_preference} labels (61.2\%), 115 (46.9\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\%) are both-dead, all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\%, within 1$\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\pm$ 0.5\%, step-level Trainer evaluation); score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 97.4\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.
95

_source line 6884 · 2026-05-19T15:17:43.719952+00:00_

22	
23	\paragraph{Framework applicability.}
24	The penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). The framework requires paired modality episodes with binary catastrophic outcomes. Preliminary pilots on MiniHack~\citep{minihack-2021} navigation tasks (0\% death rate, no monster spawning) and Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022} (100\% death rate for both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: environments lacking a death mechanism yield no routing signal (consistent with the MATH null), while environments where no strategy avoids death lack exploitable asymmetry. The framework's diagnostic power requires an intermediate regime where strategy choice meaningfully affects survival; NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks, where permadeath interacts with strategy quality, remain the most direct replication target; recent benchmarks \citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide infrastructure.
25

_source line 6888 · 2026-05-19T15:17:50.959314+00:00_

24	\paragraph{Safe RL.}
25	Safe RL methods enforce constraints in MDPs through diverse mechanisms: comprehensive surveys \citep{garcia2015comprehensive} categorize approaches; risk-sensitive objectives \citep{tamar2015cvar} optimize conditional value-at-risk; constrained policy optimization \citep{achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021} satisfies cost constraints during training; safe exploration \citep{moldovan2012safe} guarantees constraint satisfaction during learning. SafeRoute \citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails. These methods assume the agent can learn to avoid catastrophic states; our results show that in environments with irreversible failure, modality routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism---the deterministic strategy sidesteps catastrophic states entirely, achieving what constrained optimization would target.
26

46	This work uses ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a publicly available text-based simulation environment, and does not involve human subjects or private data. All evaluation environments (ScienceWorld, ALFWorld, MATH) are released under permissive open-source licenses (Apache 2.0 / MIT). All models used (Qwen2.5 family, Llama-3.1-8B) are publicly available for research use. All language models are accessed via standard APIs or publicly released weights. Experiments were conducted on 2$\times$ NVIDIA RTX 5090 (32GB) and 4$\times$ NVIDIA RTX 6000 PRO (96GB), totaling approximately 200 GPU-hours for data collection, router training, and evaluation. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.

52	\section{Signal Concentration Index}
53	\label{app:sci}
54	
55	Let $K$ denote the number of task types and $c_k$ the contribution of task type $k$ to the aggregate routing signal, defined as the fraction of total death-rate differential attributable to task $k$:
56	\begin{equation}
57	    c_k = \frac{n_k \cdot \delta_k}{\sum_{j=1}^{K} n_j \cdot \delta_j}
58	\end{equation}
59	where $n_k$ is the number of episodes and $\delta_k = d_k^{\tt int} - d_k^{\tt det}$ is the per-task death-rate differential. The SCI is the Gini coefficient over $\{c_1, \ldots, c_K\}$:
60	\begin{equation}
61	    \text{SCI} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^K \sum_{j=1}^K |c_i - c_j|}{2K \sum_{k=1}^K c_k}
62	\end{equation}
63

14	
15	Of 95 non-tie routing labels across 245 episodes, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: one strategy dies while the other survives. This label fraction is penalty-independent---it depends only on which episodes produce asymmetric deaths, not on how death is penalized. Of these 75, 54 are sole-signal labels (episodes where only one strategy produced a non-zero score): both strategies score zero without death, so death avoidance was the \emph{sole} discriminating signal. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score. At $P{=}0$, the 54 sole-signal labels reduce to ties, leaving 41 non-tie labels of which 21 (51.2\%) still involve death asymmetry; the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}), but the practical routing value of death avoidance requires $P > 0$.
16

533	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
534	\label{app:failure-modes}
535	
536	All 105 internal-strategy deaths (100\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, and applies it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer and triggers the $-100$ death penalty. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40 for deterministic) reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.
537	
538	Manual inspection identifies four failure categories: (A) no task awareness, where the agent executes \texttt{focus on} without reading the task; (B) task-aware but lacking action grounding,
…[truncated 2683 chars]

_source line 6906 · 2026-05-19T15:18:36.131668+00:00_

10	\emph{Intervention resistance.} Four axes spanning retry, self-correction, parameter scaling, and action-space restriction all fail to close the gap to deterministic safety (16.3\%): Retry ($43.5\%$ at $N{=}5$), Reflexion ($34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$, plateauing at $34$--$36.5\%$), $10{\times}$ scaling ($30.6\%$, 72B, $N{=}255$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). The strongest interventions still leave ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps; Reflexion and scaling converge to overlapping 95\% Wilson CIs ($[27.8\%, 40.8\%]$ and $[25.2\%, 36.5\%]$), suggesting a shared bound from the lack of ground-truth state access.
11	\emph{Cross-model stability.} Both Qwen-7B and Llama-8B exhibit nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\sim}$42\%), confirming that death-prone failure is structurally determined by the environment rather than model-specific; the 72B model reduces death rate but leaves death dominance intact (DAF $= 92.4\%$).
12	More aggressive planning-based interventions (Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested and may further reduce death rates; we characterize a ceiling for the tested intervention classes, not a universal bound.

13	
14	\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
15	All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (taxonomy in Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is action-space grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot ground understanding in valid primitives. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\sim}5\%$ \texttt{focus on} rate in both). \texttt{find-non-living-thing}, the sole zero-death task, uses \texttt{focus on} more frequently than any death-prone task: non-living objects are the majority class, so indiscriminate submissions rarely trigger death; lethality is thus a joint function of action misuse and answer distribution. Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\% \to 37.0\%$ via prompt warning; $\to 29.0\%$ via action-space filter), with ironic effects where warnings increased \texttt{focus on} salience on identification tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).
16	
17	\paragraph{Practitioner decision framework.}

_source line 6934 · 2026-05-19T15:21:03.608119+00:00_

1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}
5	The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable features. Cross-task-type transfer to physical-science tasks yields chance-level accuracy (49.2\%, 95\% CI $[42.4\%, 55.9\%]$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}), and 7 of 11 task types contain only deterministic-preferred labels, making majority-class collapse a structural consequence of the label distribution.
6	
7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	While death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\S\ref{sec:daf}), three properties were not predictable a priori.
9	\emph{Routing prior.} The oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\% of decisions (score improvement: 2.89 points), and even with perfect per-task death risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}). A practitioner's rational prior should therefore be ``do not route''---the oracle degeneracy means the expected value of routing development is near zero.
10	\emph{Intervention resistance.} Four axes spanning retry, self-correction, parameter scaling, and action-space restriction all fail to close the gap to deterministic safety (16.3\%): Retry ($43.5\%$ at $N{=}5$), Reflexion ($34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$, plateauing at $34$--$36.5\%$), $10{\times}$ scaling ($30.6\%$, 72B, $N{=}255$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). The strongest interventions still leave ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps; Re

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nWhile death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}), three properties were not predictable a priori.\n\\emph{Routing prior.} The oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions (score improvement: 2.89 points), and even with perfect per-task death risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}). A practitioner's rational prior should therefore be ``do not route''---the oracle degeneracy means the expected value of routing development is near zero.\n\\emph{Intervention resistance.} Four axes spanning retry, self-correction, parameter scaling, and action-space restriction all fail to close the gap to deterministic safety (16.3\\%): Retry ($43.5\\%$ at $N{=}5$), Reflexion ($34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$, plateauing at $34$--$36.5\\%$), $10{\\times}$ scaling ($30.6\\%$, 72B, $N{=}255$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). The strongest interventions still leave ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps; Reflexion and scaling converge to overlapping 95\\% Wilson CIs ($[27.8\\%, 40.8\\%]$ and $[25.2\\%, 36.5\\%]$), suggesting a shared bound from the lack of ground-truth state access.\n\\emph{Cross-model stability.} Both Qwen-7B and Llama-8B exhibit nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\\sim}$42\\%), confirming that death-prone failure is structurally determined by the environment rather than model-specific; the 72B model reduces death rate but leaves death dominance intact (DAF $= 92.4\\%$).\nMore aggressive planning-based interventions (Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested and may further reduce death rates; we characterize a ceiling for the tested intervention classes, not a universal bound.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nWhile death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}), three properties were not predictable a priori: (i)~\\emph{routing prior}---the oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions (2.89-point improvement), and threshold routing with perfect death risk knowledge still cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}), so the rational prior is ``do not route''; (ii)~\\emph{intervention resistance}---retry ($43.5\\%$), Reflexion ($34.0\\%$), $10{\\times}$ scaling ($30.6\\%$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\\%$) all leave ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps to deterministic safety ($16.3\\%$), with Reflexion and scaling converging to overlapping Wilson CIs suggesting a shared bound from lacking ground-truth state access; (iii)~\\emph{cross-model stability}---Qwen-7B and Llama-8B show nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\\sim}$42\\%), and 72B scaling leaves death dominance intact (DAF $= 92.4\\%$). More aggressive interventions (Tree-of-Thought~\\citep{tot-2023}, process reward models) remain untested; we characterize a ceiling for the tested classes, not a universal bound."
}
```

{
  "replace_all": "False",

  "old_string": "All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is action-space grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot ground understanding in valid primitives. The majority of death episodes (Category B in Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) show effective reasoning in non-fatal steps---task comprehension, environment navigation, object identification---before a single grounding error triggers irreversible failure. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both). \\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, the sole zero-death task, uses \\texttt{focus on} more frequently than any death-prone task: non-living objects are the majority class, so indiscriminate submissions rarely trigger death; lethality is thus a joint function of action misuse and answer distribution. Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 37.0\\%$ via prompt warning; $\\to 29.0\\%$ via action-space filter), with ironic effects where warnings increased \\texttt{focus on} salience on identification tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).",
  "new_string": "All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot map understanding to valid primitives; the majority of death episodes show effective reasoning in non-fatal steps before a single grounding error triggers irreversible failure. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both). \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths) uses \\texttt{focus on} more frequently than death-prone tasks---non-living objects are the majority class, so indiscriminate submissions rarely trigger death; lethality is a joint function of action misuse and answer distribution. Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 29.0\\%$ via action-space filter; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation})."
}

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Pure-strategy gap.}\nOur counterfactual labels derive from pure-strategy episodes, but a deployed router produces mixed trajectories whose state distributions differ from either pure strategy~\\citep{dagger-2011}. The 4B router's end-to-end score ($-12.03$) falling below always-deterministic ($-9.47$) may partly reflect this distribution shift rather than label noise alone. Closing this gap requires on-policy data collection under the router's own mixed policy, which is expensive given the 3.7\\% internal-preferred base rate (${\\sim}1{,}350$ paired episodes for 50 minority-class samples).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Pure-strategy gap.}\nCounterfactual labels derive from pure-strategy episodes, but a deployed router produces mixed trajectories with different state distributions~\\citep{dagger-2011}. The 4B router's end-to-end score ($-12.03$, below always-deterministic $-9.47$) may partly reflect this distribution shift. On-policy correction is expensive given the 3.7\\% internal-preferred base rate (${\\sim}1{,}350$ paired episodes for 50 minority-class samples)."
}

{

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). The framework requires paired computation mode episodes with binary catastrophic outcomes. Preliminary pilots on MiniHack~\\citep{minihack-2021} navigation tasks (0\\% death rate, no monster spawning) and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} (100\\% death rate for both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: environments lacking a death mechanism yield no routing signal (consistent with the MATH null), while environments where no strategy avoids death lack exploitable asymmetry. The framework's diagnostic power requires an intermediate regime where strategy choice meaningfully affects survival; NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks, where permadeath interacts with strategy quality, remain the most direct replication target; recent benchmarks \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide infrastructure.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Preliminary pilots on MiniHack~\\citep{minihack-2021} (0\\% death, no monster spawning) and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} (100\\% death, both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: environments lacking a death mechanism yield no routing signal (consistent with MATH), while environments where no strategy avoids death lack exploitable asymmetry. The framework requires an intermediate regime where strategy choice affects survival; NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks remain the most direct replication target \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}."
}

{
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
  "old_string": "Safe RL methods enforce constraints in MDPs through diverse mechanisms: comprehensive surveys \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive} categorize approaches; risk-sensitive objectives \\citep{tamar2015cvar} optimize conditional value-at-risk; constrained policy optimization \\citep{achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021} satisfies cost constraints during training; safe exploration \\citep{moldovan2012safe} guarantees constraint satisfaction during learning. SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails. These methods assume the agent can learn to avoid catastrophic states; our results show that in environments with irreversible failure, computation mode routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism---the deterministic strategy sidesteps catastrophic states entirely, achieving what constrained optimization would target.",
  "new_string": "Safe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} enforce constraints in MDPs via risk-sensitive objectives, constrained optimization, or safe exploration; SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails. These assume the agent can learn to avoid catastrophic states; our results show that computation mode routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism---the deterministic strategy sidesteps catastrophic states entirely."
}
```

</details>

1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}
5	The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable features. Cross-task-type transfer to physical-science tasks yields chance-level accuracy (49.2\%, 95\% CI $[42.4\%, 55.9\%]$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}), and 7 of 11 task types contain only deterministic-preferred labels, making majority-class collapse a structural consequence of the label distribution.
6	
7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	While death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\S\ref{sec:daf}), three properties were not predictable a priori: (i)~\emph{routing prior}---the oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\% of decisions (2.89-point improvement), and threshold routing with perfect death risk knowledge still cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}), so the rational prior is ``do not route''; (ii)~\emph{intervention resistance}---retry ($43.5\%$), Reflexion ($34.0\%$), $10{\times}$ scaling ($30.6\%$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\%$) all leave ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps to deterministic safety ($16.3\%$), with Reflexion and scaling converging to overlapping Wilson CIs suggesting a shared bound from lacking ground-truth state access; (iii)~\emph{cross-model stability}---Qwen-7B and Llama-8B show nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\sim}$42\%), and 72B scaling leaves death dominance intact (DAF $= 92.4\%$). _source line 6960 · 2026-05-19T15:23:01.629797+00:00_

1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	A Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.
7	Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.
8	More recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \cite{mtrouter-2026}.
9	Concurrent analyses of when and whether to invoke tools~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026} further presuppose diverse routing signals.
10	The shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.
11	But no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments. Without such a decomposition, the field cannot distinguish settings where diverse routing signals justify learned routers from tho
…[truncated 4280 chars]

_source line 6962 · 2026-05-19T15:23:14.678568+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We presented a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents via episode-level counterfactual analysis. Applied to ScienceWorld (245 episodes, 11 task types), 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry; agent improvement baselines (retry, self-correction, $10{\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) confirm this is robust. Cross-domain contrasts support framework predictions: MATH shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism. Future work should target environments with intermediate death regimes, such as NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks and Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022} with tuned difficulty, building on recent LLM agent benchmarks \citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}; evaluation with stronger backbones is another direct open direction.
4

69	We also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}), reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
70	
71	\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}
72	The 97.4\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) means episode-level comparison is the only granularity at which routing signal emerges. This design choice has a structural consequence: the framework outputs a binary diagnostic (``is this environment death-dominated?'') rather than step-level routing decisions. When episode-level labels are projected back to steps for router training (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}), ${\sim}27.7\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with episode outcomes, quantifying the information loss inherent in this projection. The framework is thus a diagnostic tool for characterizing when routing adds value, not a step-level routing optimizer.
73

  "old_string": "\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{An episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} for decomposing routing signal, with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics and router training as a diagnostic probe (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:daf}, \\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Evidence that death dominance resists standard interventions}: 78.9\\% of non-tie labels arise from death asymmetry; four representative axes (retry, self-correction, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) all fail to close the gap, with action filtering and 72B scaling providing the strongest yet insufficient reductions (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-environment boundary probes}: MATH ($p = 0.81$) confirms the predicted null; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry; a Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms cross-model consistency with 10/10 direction consistency (\\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\\end{enumerate}",

  "old_string": "We presented a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents via episode-level counterfactual analysis. Applied to ScienceWorld (245 episodes, 11 task types), 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry; agent improvement baselines (retry, self-correction, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) confirm this is robust. Cross-domain contrasts support framework predictions: MATH shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism. Future work should target environments with intermediate death regimes, such as NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} with tuned difficulty, building on recent LLM agent benchmarks \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}; evaluation with stronger backbones is another direct open direction.",
  "new_string": "We presented a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents via episode-level counterfactual analysis. Applied to ScienceWorld (245 episodes, 11 task types), 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry; four intervention axes confirm this is robust. Cross-domain probes support framework predictions: MATH confirms the null without catastrophic failure; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry. Future work should target environments with intermediate death regimes (NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat, Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} with tuned difficulty) and stronger backbones."
}

    "old_string": "We presented a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents via episode-level counterfactual analysis. Applied to ScienceWorld (245 episodes, 11 task types), 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry; agent improvement baselines (retry, self-correction, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) confirm this is robust. Cross-domain contrasts support framework predictions: MATH shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism. Future work should target environments with intermediate death regimes, such as NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} with tuned difficulty, building on recent LLM agent benchmarks \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}; evaluation with stronger backbones is another direct open direction.",
    "new_string": "We presented a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents via episode-level counterfactual analysis. Applied to ScienceWorld (245 episodes, 11 task types), 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry; four intervention axes confirm this is robust. Cross-domain probes support framework predictions: MATH confirms the null without catastrophic failure; ALFWorld identifies information asymmetry. Future work should target environments with intermediate death regimes (NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat, Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} with tuned difficulty) and stronger backbones."

_source line 6988 · 2026-05-19T15:25:00.717705+00:00_

104	
105	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic, an \emph{oracle degeneracy}: the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.
106	
107	\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}
108	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
109	
110	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; ${\sim}27.7\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with episode-level assignments, further limiting learnability. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes. Training and validation use episode-level splits (Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode information leakage.
111	
112	\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\%$--$800\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\ref{app:ablations}).
113

### Tool result

_source line 7008 · 2026-05-19T15:26:36.470376+00:00_

15	Of 95 non-tie routing labels across 245 episodes, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: one strategy dies while the other survives. This label fraction is penalty-independent---it depends only on which episodes produce asymmetric deaths, not on how death is penalized. Of these 75, 54 are sole-signal labels (episodes where only one strategy produced a non-zero score): both strategies score zero without death, so death avoidance was the \emph{sole} discriminating signal. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score. At $P{=}0$, the 54 sole-signal labels reduce to ties, leaving 41 non-tie labels of which 21 (51.2\%) still involve death asymmetry; the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}), but the practical routing value of death avoidance requires $P > 0$.
16	
17	Under ScienceWorld's penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the full score gap is $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies with penalty magnitude ($50.4\%$ at $P{=}10$; $70.8\%$ at $P{=}25$; $82.6\%$ at $P{=}50$; $90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$; Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\% label fraction provides the more robust, penalty-independent characterization. Given $\delta = 0.265$ and $P = 100$, the predicted DAF $= \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}) = 26.5 / 29.37 \approx 90.3\%$, closely matching the observed value; DAF is thus a derived quantity once $\delta$, $P$, and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ are known.

19	\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}
20	The internal strategy dies in 42.9\% of episodes versus 16.3\% for deterministic (2.6$\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\Delta_{\text{full}} = P \cdot \delta + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$ makes $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\delta$ with residual $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \leq 25$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \texttt{det\_preferred}, 9 \texttt{internal\_preferred}, and 150 \texttt{no\_preference}.
21

78	\paragraph{MATH.} On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$, code interpreter as deterministic computation mode), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp), with balanced labels (107 vs.\ 95, 74.6\% no-preference). Without catastrophic failure, no routing signal emerges.
79	
80	\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\citep{alfworld-2021} (6 task types, 135 games) provides the deterministic strategy with admissible actions, an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch. The success rate gap is $27.4\%$ vs.\ $3.0\%$ (35 det-preferred, 2 int-preferred, 98 no-preference). Of 37 non-tie labels, 35 (94.6\%) favor the information-advantaged strategy, a one-directional dominance analogous to ScienceWorld's death dominance but driven by information asymmetry rather than catastrophic failure. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism without modification.
81

_source line 7014 · 2026-05-19T15:26:56.411233+00:00_

104	
105	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic, an \emph{oracle degeneracy}: the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.
106	
107	\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}
108	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
109	
110	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; ${\sim}27.7\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with episode-level assignments, further limiting learnability. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes. Training and validation use episode-level splits (Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode information leakage.
111	
112	\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\%$--$800\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\ref{app:ablations}).
113
